  The alert is right and what was written about it was wrong. M4b's third row, the rule's own
  comment, PRODUCT.md, the tool's description and two skills all said the alert is for the case
  of 2026-10-06, "a .NET service that answered 500 for hours inside its 256Mi". That service fails
  with its container at 56% of the limit: .NET caps its heap at 75% of the container limit and
  counts what it has committed, not what is resident. No threshold on a container's memory tells
  that from a healthy service, so the alert (above 90% for 15 minutes) cannot fire for it and never
  could have. What does see it is the error rate: `OrbitIngress5xxRateHigh` was firing. And the
  same blindness sits in the advice: from memory use that service has room to spare, and
  `orbit_size_recommend` would suggest lowering it, which shrinks the heap further.
  Fixed in v0.34.23, nothing about what the alert measures: the rule's comment and description,
  PRODUCT.md ("What memory numbers cannot tell"), the tool's description and the change-service
  skill say what the alert and the advice cannot see and what does; a suggestion to LOWER memory
  ends with the caution and what to check; S06e ships the engine a variant that really sits at 93%
  of its limit, alive (a new switch in the test tenant, `FILL_MEMORY_PERCENT`, which holds memory
  outside the managed heap: built and run in a 256 MiB container, working set 92.9%, not killed).
  **For the owner, in plain words (also §12):** the alert you approved for M4b does what it says on
  a workload that is truly at its container limit. It does not catch the incident that made us

  when the second pass of the suite lost two more scenarios to it (S02: the bot's ship failed at
  the registry step after api and engine had landed; S18: the same for engine). The registry was
  never the trouble: 40 of 40 requests from the node and 60 of 60 from inside the orbitd pod
  answered. The cluster's own DNS was:
  ```
  $ kubectl -n kube-system logs deploy/coredns --since=3h | grep -c -i 'error\|timeout\|unreachable'
  285
  [ERROR] plugin/errors: 2 checkers-lab.example.com. A: dial udp [2a01:4ff:ff00::add:1]:53: connect: network is unreachable
  [ERROR] plugin/errors: 2 checkers-lab.example.com. A: dial udp [2a01:4ff:ff00::add:2]:53: connect: network is unreachable
  $ cat /run/systemd/resolve/resolv.conf        (the node)
  nameserver 2a01:4ff:ff00::add:2
  nameserver 2a01:4ff:ff00::add:1
  nameserver 185.12.64.1
  nameserver 185.12.64.2
  ```
  CoreDNS forwards every name outside the cluster to the nameservers of the kubelet's
  resolv.conf, which is the node's; the provider's image lists two IPv6 resolvers first; the pod
  network has no IPv6 route. Whenever CoreDNS picked one of the two, the lookup failed, and
  whatever was asking just then failed with it: a ship's registry check, a backup's bucket name
  (the same log has timeouts for the object store's name). Every node Orbit builds on this
  provider has it; the server before the rebuild was installed in August from an image that
  evidently did not. Fixed in v0.34.24: `bootstrap-node.sh` writes the node's IPv4 resolvers to
  `/etc/rancher/k3s/resolv.conf`, gives it to the kubelet (`--resolv-conf`), re-runs the k3s
  installer when the unit lacks the flag, and restarts CoreDNS when the list changed; a node with
  no IPv4 resolver keeps the default and says so. verify stage2 gains a row: no `network is
  unreachable` in CoreDNS's log of the last ten minutes, red when the log cannot be read. The
  three asks of v0.34.22 stay: a registry can also be away for a moment.
  - **Proof required:** offline, `scripts/test_bootstrap_node_arch.py` (the kubelet is given the
    node's IPv4 resolvers only and CoreDNS is restarted; a node with none keeps the default and says
    so), `scripts/test_verify_runner.py` (the row green on a clean log, red on three unreachable
    lines without repeating them, red when the log cannot be read). Live: `bootstrap-node.sh` run
    again on the rebuilt box at v0.34.24; an hour later CoreDNS's log holds no `network is
    unreachable` and no forward timeout; `make verify` shows the row green; the counted §5 run has
    no job ending `failed at step registry`.
  - Evidence. Offline, both whole suites before v0.34.32: `scripts suite: 1342 passed, 0 failed`, `2512 passed, 13 skipped`. Live, the rebuilt box (bootstrap run again on it at v0.34.24, and again by
    S16 of the counted run: `ok    a node bootstrap re-run changes nothing (exit 0, k3s already
    running with current args, not restarted)`):

    ```
    nameserver 185.12.64.1
    nameserver 185.12.64.2
    ```

  The restore had printed `RESTORED: demo-prod/demo-db holds the data of <name>` and the games
  were not back. Not the test: backup names are stamped to the second, and the backup a
  restore takes first ("the state before") was stamped in the same second as the backup
  being restored. It overwrote it, on the volume and in the bucket, and the restore loaded
  the emptied state and compared it with itself. The laptop had just been given memory back
  and ran the fixture inside one second; a real database takes longer to dump, so this has
  not happened on a box, and a backup must not be overwritable at any speed. `db-backup.sh`
  now waits out the second when the name exists.

  The new pod is refused, so the old one is never removed, so the room never comes back.
  S02's and S18's ships to dev then ended `LANDED NOT DEPLOYED rollout`, rightly. It did not
  clear by itself: deleting the old pod brings it back (its ReplicaSet is still wanted), and a
  quota raised by hand is put back by the reconcile loop before the refused ReplicaSet tries
  again. It took a raised quota AND the two refused ReplicaSets deleted in the same minute
  (done on the test box at 15:47Z, to give the rest of the run a namespace to work in). Any
  operator who follows the sizing advice downwards by more than the quota's spare room meets
  this, and S22 exists to make an agent do exactly that. Fixed in v0.34.30: orbitd raises a quota
  at once and lowers it only when the pair's Deployments are live as rendered and done
  rolling; until then every key keeps the higher of the live and the wanted value (the room
  the old pods were admitted under), and the reconcile pass after the rollout lowers it.

  component was ready for 95.8% of the window, so the window does show what a healthy run
  uses. The rule (three restarts without a kill: never shrink) read a count as a crash loop,

  advice to fix what was already fixed. Fixed in v0.34.32: the tool also reads the share of the
  window the component was ready; the refusal stands under 90% or when the share is not
  known, and says the share; at 90% or more the advice is given and says why it is despite
  the restarts.

  Nothing was lost: the box, its Prometheus and its bucket are three days old, so there is no
  data six or thirteen days back to keep. And the three "expired" rows passed for the same
  reason, which proves nothing. The driver asked for fixed ages and its own header said it
  was for "a box older than the longest window"; nothing in it knew the box's age. It now
  reads the node's creation time: "kept" is asked at the oldest moment the store can be asked
  about (the window less a day, or the box's age less three hours), and "expired" is judged
  only on a box older than the window plus a day; before that the row is printed as not
  judged, with the day it can be, and counts as neither pass nor fail.
  **What this leaves unproven on the rebuilt box, said plainly:** that data is dropped at the
  end of each window. That can first be seen there on 2026-10-12 (Tempo), 2026-10-16
  (Prometheus) and 2026-10-23 (Loki). It was seen on the box before the rebuild, older than
  every window, on 2026-10-07 (`retention: 11 passed, 0 failed`, §6), and the retention
  settings the rebuilt box runs are the same rendered ones (the rows that read the live
  configuration pass: `7d`, `retention_period: 2w`, `block_retention: 72h0m0s`).

  as rendered and done rolling", and "as rendered" compared the whole object. A Deployment
  that was ever restarted through `orbit_deploy_restart` keeps the restart's stamp for good, so
  it is never as rendered again, and its namespace's quota is never lowered again. The counted
  run did not see it: S22's teardown ran at 05:20Z, a minute before that restart. The harm is
  small and the fix of v0.34.30 was wrong in this: a quota that stays at an older, larger
  value still admits everything the catalog asks for, and is no longer the ceiling the catalog
  derives. Fixed in v0.34.34: settled compares what the pods ask for (each container's requests
  and limits, as quantities), which is what a quota counts, and the rollout's state.

## 9. V2 (out of scope for V1 — recorded, not built)

- Automatic fitting of service resources (owner, 2026-10-07: not in V1). Decided for when it is
  built: opt-in, `fit: off | recommend | apply`; a FLOOR per service from a first sizing and a
  CEILING from a load test, the fitter moving only between them; memory AND CPU; a service with no
  load test gets advice only; a panel on each service's default dashboard showing the progression
  of its use and what the fitter did; a 24 h recommend-only run before `apply`; the decision engine
  as a small Rust binary, the commit and the guardrails in orbitd. Worth it at about fifty services
  or several nodes, where nobody looks at each service and tighter packing removes a node; at that
  size the Kubernetes Vertical Pod Autoscaler's recommender (500Mi requested by its stock manifest,
  too much for one small node) is a candidate to switch on rather than rebuild. Initial sizing by
  load test (`orbit size`: the exact image, three load levels of a short declared request mix, or
  the mix rebuilt from recorded per-route traffic) belongs with it. Research of 2026-10-07 (primary
  sources): no managed platform measures before a first deploy; the products that size by load test
  never invent the load; nobody derives the endpoint mix.

In-cluster builds and an orbitd build tool · push-to-deploy · on-box image registry ·
agents acting on their own (intake, agent runtime, notifier) · console UI · context lake ·
functions, buckets-as-a-feature, status page · multi-node · cloud/DNS provider abstraction ·
git hosts other than GitHub · external dead-man heartbeat · preview environments.

Found in V1 and left for V2, with the workaround V1 lives with:
- k3s's network policy controller adds a new pod to its rule sets on its next sync, so a pod's first
  seconds are under no egress rule (a narrowed component can reach the internet briefly at start).
  Measured by S12's probes (they wait 45 s); a CNI with synchronous policy is the V2 fix.
- Platform apply does not revert hand-added fields the way the tenant applier now does (§8): a hand
  edit to a platform object shows as drift in the plan and is the operator's to undo.
- Protecting main of the instance repository on every instance (a ruleset the onboarding creates:
  pull requests only, Orbit's App on the bypass list). Today Orbit copes with such a rule where an
  organization made one (§8, 2026-10-08) and makes none itself.
- A way to take a base backup of a production database now (a tool, or the restore doing it when
  there is none): today the first one is taken when the database is made and then on the nightly
  schedule, and a database whose first one failed has no point in time to restore to until the
  next night (§8, 2026-10-08 14:45Z and 2026-10-09 01:08Z).

## 10. Decision log

- 2026-10-07 — Owner: automatic fitting is cut from V1 (the driver's recommendation, after three
  research reports from primary sources and the question "is it needed at this scale?"). M4b becomes
  sizing ADVICE: a read-only recommendation tool, a documented default with the quota and the fit
  check as the limits, and an alert for a workload stuck at its memory limit. The earlier decisions of
  the day on the fitter's shape stand for V2 (§9). The goal's "M4b verification bar" is this
  milestone's three proofs; "M4b's design approved" is this decision. Facts the decision rests on:
  one 16 GB node, services of about 256Mi, so perfect fitting frees well under 1 GiB; the box's
  Kubernetes (v1.36.4) resizes a pod in place, so a human or agent applying a size costs no restart
  policy work; Google documents its own vertical autoscaler as "not ready" for JVM-style runtimes;
  .NET caps its heap at 75% of the container limit, which is how the engine failed inside 256Mi.

- 2026-10-07 — Owner, on M4b's shape (the full design is still to be written and approved before any
  of it is built): (1) the first sizing sets a FLOOR per service (below it the fitter never goes, so
  it cannot kill a process by accident) and a CEILING defined by the load test; the fitter's room is
  between the two. (2) Memory AND CPU are fitted. (3) When fitting is on, each service's default
  Grafana dashboard shows the progression of its memory and CPU use (with what the fitter did).
  (4) The recommend-only run on the box is 24 hours (was "several days"). (5) Asked whether the
  fitter can be a Rust program: to be answered in the design (the driver's proposal: the decision
  engine as a small Rust binary, the commit and the guardrails staying in orbitd; put to the owner,
  no objection). (6) V1.0 ships `apply` as supported, once the whole bar passes. (7) A service with no
  load test has no ceiling and gets recommendations only, never an automatic change.

- 2026-10-06 — Owner: no CI. Every GitHub Actions workflow is removed from core (the tests job and
  the four image-build jobs) and the optional Actions build pipeline leaves the template; tests run on
  the operator laptop (`scripts/local-tests.sh`). To go faster: a change is held to the tests it
  touches and the whole suite runs once per release; related fixes share a PR; evidence in this file
  goes straight to `main`; after a fix only the affected scenarios are re-run, the whole §5 and §6
  suite once on the final release. The minimal-profile run is 6 hours, not 24. The eight pull requests
  from before V1 are closed (Vault and webhook work on code that is gone; #58's defect was fixed in
  #200; #63 and #176 are superseded by this file). M4b (auto-fitting) stays last and is not drafted
  early.

- 2026-10-04 — V1 = operate, not drive. Finish line §1. No new orbitd tools. Local builds. All onboarding inputs up front. Owner OK with downtime on the test box and with removing the `esc-*` apps. Driver has full authority incl. merges. Observability tested fully (§6); no external heartbeat. Telegram delivery proven by a recording receiver + counters. checkers-lab gets three components so traces cross services.

- 2026-10-04 — Owner: copy the test box's sops age key from the cluster (`sops/sops-age`) into the desk's credentials directory, for this box only (M4 mints a fresh key into the Recovery Kit). Owner: re-bootstrap orbitd for a desk credential; the September admin principal is retired (store backed up on the node at /root/orbitd-backup-20261004/ and on the PVC as principals.json.pre-v1-20261004).

- 2026-10-05 — Regression strategy for §5/§6 (M2 "wired into `make verify`"): a scenario needs a fresh agent,
  minutes of wall time and faults planted in a live tenant, so it cannot be a `make verify` stage that
  runs on every verify. Instead: (1) `make verify` stage7-scenarios re-checks, with no agent and no fault,
  the standing properties the scenarios proved (the seven guardrails with throwaway principals, tenant
  isolation, every door's http→https); (2) each fixed defect has an offline test in CI; (3)
  `scenarios/run-all.sh` re-runs the whole suite with agents before a release and after M4's rebuild.

- 2026-10-05 — Owner: V1 adds M2c (opt-in features, the commit gate, guardrail tools; design
  presented in the session and approved) and M4b (auto-fitting, last, design deferred, verification
  led by the driver). Agents operate Orbit and no PR is reviewed by a person; new orbitd tools are
  added on real need. Order: M2b -> M2c -> M3 updates (features in the form, bucket-less path) -> M4
  -> M4b -> M5.

- 2026-10-05 — Owner, M4: the onboarding form is assembled from the existing credentials file to file
  (never shown), adopting server 12345678 with `rebuild: true`; the old instance is renamed aside, not
  deleted (GitHub repo -> example-platform-pre-m4, local checkout and credentials directory
  -> *.pre-m4), so the run starts from a fresh repo and a fresh credentials directory.

- 2026-10-06 — M2c, how a feature is switched in the cluster. orbitd's own ServiceAccount keeps the
  tenant kinds and nothing more; platform Applications (CRDs, namespaces, cluster roles, webhooks)
  are applied by a short-lived Job under a second ServiceAccount, `orbit-platform`, bound to
  cluster-admin, which orbitd may only start and read (Role `orbitd-converge-jobs` in `orbit`).
  Reasons: rendering a platform chart does not fit orbitd's 192Mi limit; the Job survives an orbitd
  restart; orbitd's long-lived token is not widened. What this does NOT bound: whoever controls
  orbitd can already commit to the instance repo, and the Job applies what that repo renders, so a
  compromised orbitd reaches the platform through a commit. The bounds on that path are the admin
  role, the confirmation round, the ledger trailers, and the commit gate (M2c, next). An admission
  policy pinning the Job's shape was considered and not built: it would not close the commit path.
- 2026-10-06 — M2c, a feature's requirement is read as config (a bucket declared), another feature,
  and the sealed secret it needs both in git and applied (`secrets:` in the registry, e.g.
  `loki/orbit-s3`); orbitd reads the operator's SopsSecret objects (ciphertext), never Secrets.

  `main-b3b0d07` 1/1, throwaway workloads and the disk file gone, prod 200). The §6 drivers also wait on
  the owner's word to restart. The laptop also runs
  an unrelated local Supabase stack (six containers, up 34 h) that is not Orbit's to stop.

- RESOLVED 2026-10-05 23:47Z: the Loki delete request (§8, the info-level query log) shows
  `processed` and the deleted window holds 0 matching lines (output under M2b's §6 row).

- RESOLVED 2026-10-04: `write:packages` on the operator laptop's `gh` (`gh auth status` lists it).
- M4 only: the §7 form values and the two GitHub App clicks.
- RESOLVED 2026-10-06: GitHub Actions minutes are no longer a risk: there is no CI (§2, §10).
  Was: risk, not a blocker: GitHub Actions minutes. Actions refused every job from 2026-09-15 for billing ("recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased") and ran again in October. CI is one `tests` job per PR to spend as little as possible; if Actions stops again, merges stop (spending is a hard stop) and this line becomes a blocker. Making orbit-core public would make Actions free — the owner's call.
- RESOLVED 2026-10-04: the operator laptop now holds the test box's age key, copied from the cluster on the owner's decision (§10); M4 mints a fresh key into the Recovery Kit.

## 13. After V1: the QA of v1.0.0, and v1.0.1 to v1.0.4 (2026-10-12)

The owner asked for a QA of v1.0.0 "as a curious user", and then for every finding to be
fixed and shown fixed, with no regression. Not part of the V1 finish line (§1), recorded
here because §8's rule holds: a fix is proven on the box, and a tool added on the way is
written down.

**The QA.** A second project, `guestbook` (a python page, a worker, a scheduled job, a
database; dev and prod), was onboarded onto the rebuilt test box by the documented path and
used for four hours: ships good and bad, rollbacks, monitors, a crash loop, an
out-of-memory kill, an accidental wipe and its restore, hand edits, a feature switched off
and on, the three roles, a reboot, removal, re-creation, removal. Forty findings, F1 to F40.

**The fixes.** orbit-core #294 to #297, released as v1.0.1 after three release candidates
on the box. The regression runs then found three more things, one release each: #298
(v1.0.2), #299 (v1.0.3), #300 (v1.0.4). docs/CHANGELOG.md has each. Every finding was
re-tested on the box by repeating what had shown it; the last whole pass ran on v1.0.3.

### The forty findings

Notes on the table. Four lines of the last pass read FAIL before the line shown:

- F9, twice: my own Alertmanager silence, put on for the pass over every alert named `gb-*`, hid the test monitor's alert from `orbit_alerts_active` while the monitor read `firing` and Alertmanager held it as suppressed. Asked again with the silence narrowed: listed in 11 s.
- F30: the pass's first guestbook showed the backup note of a guestbook removed under v1.0.1-rc3, before a removal dropped the note. Removed and made again on v1.0.3: no note.
- F33: `recent_hand_edits` of a guestbook made again carried the hand edits undone for the one removed an hour earlier. That is the fix of v1.0.4, shown on v1.0.4 in the F33 row.

### Tools added (§8: a tool is added when the work shows the need, and recorded)

| tool | role | the need |
|---|---|---|
| `orbit_job_cancel` | operator | F18: a ship waiting ten minutes for a rollout that will not finish held its project/environment, and nothing could stop it |
| `orbit_monitor_unsilence` | operator | F15: a silence could be made and never lifted |
| `orbit_catalog_history` | viewer | F31: a change of configuration was in no history a tool could read |

45 tools in all: a viewer has 26, an operator 36, an admin 45.

### What the re-tests themselves showed

Found on the box while re-testing, each fixed and covered by a test:

| fixed in | what was seen | the fix |
|---|---|---|
| v1.0.1-rc2 | a metric name with a hyphen broke the feature-memory query (F39) | the name is escaped |
| v1.0.1-rc2 | between two crashes of a crash loop the status read `progressing` (F17) | what the pod has done counts, not only what it is doing |
| v1.0.1-rc2 | a firing log monitor read `inactive` | a log monitor's state is read from the Loki ruler |
| v1.0.1-rc2 | a re-created project's first tick called every object "deleted and restored" | an inventory older than its namespace is no evidence; a removal deletes the inventory |
| v1.0.1-rc2 | three out-of-memory kills inside five minutes counted as one (F21) | kills are counted minute by minute |
| v1.0.1-rc3 | a rollback that started no pod failed `log` because the old pod had been quiet | a reused quiet pod is quiet, not broken (numbered F41 in the results) |
| v1.0.1-rc3 | the last log monitor's rule stayed in the Loki ruler's ConfigMap after its removal | the ConfigMap is written empty |
| v1.0.1 | a project made again showed the removed project's backup as its own | the removal drops the note; the files stay |
| v1.0.2 | `orbit_ledger_get` and `orbit_deploy_history` listed every project's ships for every pair: checkers-lab dev's newest thirty entries were guestbook's (found by scenario S11, which stopped at its setup) | both list only the commits that changed what was asked about |
| v1.0.3 | a ship refused at the registry said `manifest not found` and no more; the agent of scenario S01, having mistyped one image, looked for the fault in orbitd | the refusal names the image, the tag and the component |
| v1.0.4 | a project made again showed the removed project's hand edits | the loop forgets a pair the catalog no longer carries |

### Not changed, and why

- **F28** (requests hanging through a crash): not reproduced on any pass. Left as it is.
- **A legacy MCP handshake.** Once, a desk call fell back to an older protocol revision and orbitd logged `handler for 'tools/call' returned an invalid result` (the confirmation request has no shape in that revision). Nothing was written and the call was repeated. Seen once; not chased.
- **Leftovers of the QA, on purpose:** the repository `example-org/guestbook` and its image package; guestbook's database backup files on the backups volume and in the bucket (a removal keeps them); the deliberate refused commits in the instance repository's history; the tags and images `v1.0.1-rc1` to `-rc3`, `v1.0.1`, `v1.0.2`, `v1.0.3`.

### The tallies

- **Local tests** (`scripts/local-tests.sh`, in chunks), on the code of v1.0.4: orbitd 2588 passed, scripts 1357 passed, 0 failed.
- **The forty findings**, re-tested on the box on v1.0.3 and, for what v1.0.4 changed, on v1.0.4: 33 of them have checks on the box (34 with F41), six are documents, one (F14) is a row of `make verify`. Results: 69 lines PASS, 4 lines FAIL (the notes above), none left open.
- **The agent scenarios** (§5), all 28, box on v1.0.2 (S15 first: an agent upgraded the box from v1.0.1-rc3 to v1.0.2, then the other 27 ran on it): 26 PASS. S22 FAIL: the agent did the work through the tools (7 of 8 checks) and also ran `echo noop` in a shell, which the scenario forbids. S01 FAIL: the mistyped image above. Both run again on v1.0.3: PASS (S22 8 of 8, S01 7 of 7). Not run again on v1.0.4, whose one change is covered by its test and the F33 row.
- **`make verify`** on v1.0.4, box back to checkers-lab only: stage2 27, stage3 411, stage4 10, stage5-platform 92, stage6-live 3, stage7-scenarios 19, 0 failed, `verify: all stages passed`. With the test project and three monitors present, on v1.0.3: 28, 421, 10, 96, 3, 21, 0 failed.
- **The box at the end:** orbitd v1.0.4, one project (checkers-lab), no monitor, no silence, Watchdog the only alert.
